ParlaMint-DK_20170202101048	The debate is open. Mrs. Julie Skovsby.
ParlaMint-DK_20170202101113	Thank you. The draft law gives us a new main law which lays down the framework for dealing with cases of scientific infidelity and cases of questionable research practice. Precisely the distinction between cases of scientific infidelity and cases of questionable research practice is very important in the draft law, since, according to the draft law, cases of scientific infidelity are to be dealt with centrally by the Scientific Injustice Board and the individual research institutions are to deal with cases of questionable research practice. The bill is based on 12 recommendations on international practice by the Oddershede Committee and focuses on the process of handling breaches of responsible research practices by establishing, as I said, the framework for dealing with cases of scientific infidelity and cases is questionable research practice. With respect for, among others, on the other hand, the Danish Code of Integrity in Research does not in itself regulate what is responsible research practice. As in the past, it is expected that the concept of responsible research practice will be developed over time in the research environments and that the research institutions will take responsibility for continuously informing their research staff about the policies and procedures applicable to the institution. I agree with the many consultation responses which state that it is neither possible nor desirable in the legislative proposal to define perfectly clear, unambiguous and finally developed definitions. On second thought, this often applies regardless of the professional skills we speak of a carpenter, a nurse, a schoolteacher, etc. Everyone has difficulty in describing what high professionalism is to us, who do not share their profession, but they themselves are not in doubt when they see actions falling under or beside their ethical standards, just as they are not in doubt when their profession develops and reaches new heights. Today, the three committees of the Committees on Scientific Integrity may deal with scientific products prepared on a private basis if the private company or the like has wanted to be covered by the committee's competences or wishes to contribute to the disclosure of the matter. However, the draft law limits the scope of the law to cases relating to research carried out with full or partial public support. Research carried out solely for private funds is therefore not covered by the draft law. In other words, if the conduct of research carried out privately for private funds is questioned, it will be up to the company concerned to take steps to investigate the matter itself. It is clear from the comments that the amendment includes, among other things: It should be seen that cases of scientific infidelity can be quite extensive and complicated to settle and it is not considered reasonable to use public resources to provide private matters. In my view, this is certainly a serious argument, just as the argument that research in the pharmaceutical industry is already regulated in very detailed terms by other legislation has to be said to be reasonable. However, we are still concerned about the question: how is it ensured that private-funded private research is properly assessed? That is why, in the committee's examination of the draft law, we will try to clarify that question further. In addition, when we look at the consultation answers and notes, we find here, which deals with the scope of the law, and on a number of other points wordings such as that the draft law has been revised in line with the comments and several of the more concrete observations are included in the revised draft law, as well as many of the consultation answers have led to clarifications in the comments. I see this as an expression of a good consultation process and, with those comments, the Social Democratic Party can support the bill. I would also like to welcome the rapporteur, Mr SF. Jacob Mark, and tell the Danish Parliament that SF also supports the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202101557	Thank you. This bill is actually very good. Over recent years there have been various cases of scientific infidelity, where there have been discussions both outside and within scientific circles as to whether the decisions are correct, whether the system, the basis is as it should be. This is precisely why a new set of rules is currently in place and has been created on the basis of recommendations from the so-called Oddershede Committee of 2015. It is a set of rules that are also parallel to systems that exist in the world, for example. in the United States. What is crucial about such a set of rules as this is that the rules should be clear and transparent and, above all, recognised and accepted in scientific circles. It must be the guarantor that any decisions on cases are valid and reliable, and I believe that this system will prove to be able to live up to that. I'm building that, among other things. That the consultation answers are largely positive and that the proposal has taken into account to a large extent the criticisms raised in the consultations. The proposal also includes annual reports from the Scientific Integrity Board, which I look forward to receiving in committee so that we can follow the implementation and application of the law in the coming years. All in all, this is an excellent proposal which the Danish People's Party can support.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202101739	Thank you. In the Enhedslisten, we are fundamentally positive about this bill. It is good to keep an eye on whether we have good research standards. Scientific malpractice can have very serious consequences in its extremes, which is why it is good that we have a framework for it. We have also noted that, in relation to the original draft proposal for a proposal on scientific maladministration, part of the legislative proposal has been amended. For example: such a dubious concept as self-aggression has been given to the draft law, and we see this as something positive. I would like to make a few comments on some of the paragraphs. Paragraph 2 of the bill on the scope of the law restricts it to publicly funded research at a public Danish research institution. Why this restriction? There is also research at the public Danish research institutions, which are privately funded, and research funded by foreign funds. In our view, they should also be covered by the law. It is not acceptable for us to operate in this way with different standards depending on the source of funding. No. 2, No. 1, deals with things that are not covered by scientific infidelity. We do not understand why this shortcut is needed. Counterfeiting, as it says, of results is certainly unacceptable in any case, regardless of whether or not they are less important for specific research. In Section 13, para. No. 1 3 and 4 states that it is possible to refuse to deal in substance with cases if the costs are not proportionate to the importance of the case or if the case has a very small link with Denmark. The Enhedslisten is not sure that these two considerations should justify that cases are not dealt with in substance. It doesn't sound right to have that sort of thing in the legal text, and in practice it can be difficult to manage, or it can become a small, uncontrollable loophole. A small page note of the bill could be whether there is sufficient legal certainty for the individual researcher. We know, of course, that a decision on scientific misconduct can have very far-reaching consequences for a researcher, and that is why we should perhaps consider whether there should not be an appeal body in the law, so that there is an opportunity for the researcher to have his case tried because, as I have said, it can have quite far-reaching consequences for the researcher, not only in the specific case, but also for the researcher's further work in general. But with these comments I would say that we are in favour of it and will try in committee to see whether we can obtain a majority in order perhaps to change a few of the points I have mentioned here.

There I think that the Oddershede Committee has actually done a very good job, so it is the serious cases that are being hit, but where it can be said that the cases where one disagrees or where there is in fact more need of scientific self-justice, one avoids the judicialisation of scientific disagreement and uses the means of self-justice that are actually in the scientific community. A good job has been done by the Oddershede Committee. ParlaMint-DK_20170202103345	Thank you. Then it's the Minister of Education and Research. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170202103354	Thank you for the fine debate that has been going on. The aim of the bill is to build on the great commitment to the integrity of research, which the Danish research environments have prioritized in recent years. The bill shall contribute to increased focus on the integrity in question. This should be done by creating a modern framework both for dealing with cases of scientific infidelity and for dealing with cases of questionable research practice. In December 2015, 12 recommendations were made by the Oddershede Committee for the adjustment of the Danish system of unfairness. The draft law is based on the recommendations of the Committee. We are calling for closer regulation of cases of scientific infidelity. The proposal proposes the creation of a central board with sole competence to deal with the cases in question, and an important part of the legislative proposal is the greater role of research institutions. They must therefore be more closely involved in the cases of maladministration at the central board. The draft law also regulates, as something new, the framework for dealing with cases of questionable research practices. The responsibility for the concrete handling lies best with the institutions, and it is therefore laid down that it is the duty of the institutions to deal with cases of dubious research practices. Firstly, the allocation of responsibilities should contribute to an increased focus on responsible research practices within each institution. Secondly, the proposal will lead to a more effective handling of cases of actual scientific misconduct in the central board. I therefore thank you for your comments and look forward to the further consideration of the draft law.

No. 2, No. 4, you can see that there is a requirement that there should be experience in the Danish Research and Innovation Policy Council with the application of commercialisation of research results. We do not believe that such a requirement is a necessary condition in relation to that body. Section 14 states that the Danish Free Research Fund grants grants to specific research activities in the fields of politically determined themes or instruments, when special funding is provided for this in the annual grant law. This paragraph could be a slippery one compared to the whole idea of free research funds, but the last part of the paragraph, which stresses that only money is included in the annual budget law, can hopefully be a stop for this. The Board of the Danish Free Research Foundation may appoint up to 75 members of the standing committees. They shall appoint both the members of the committee and the chairmen of the standing committees. Perhaps it would be more engaging if the standing committees themselves chose their presidents or if the board elected the chairman on the recommendation of the standing committee. In the legislation in force, these committees are referred to as trade committees. We hope that it is understood that the standing committees in their essence are still professional committees. I would also like to mention that a hearing from the University of Aalborg states that 75 people are too few. They believe that this figure should be increased to 90 on the basis of an estimate of the total workload. It may be difficult for me to assess whether it is correct, but it is a relationship we should try to talk about. In section 28, paragraph 1. 3 states that an appropriation from the Research Fund may be allocated to a natural or legal person. Allocation to a legal entity rather than to a specific researcher may be a slippery path which may lead to the use of research fund funds for the general operation of the institutions. When the draft law does not contain a legal entity, but says a legal person, it can be interpreted positively that it is not intended either. That's what we're gonna do. We are of the opinion that that paragraph should be deleted. There should not be a waiting period because a researcher has made an application material that is too bad. It should not be particularly time-consuming to reject such applications, and this should not mean that the person concerned cannot apply again and has a waiting period of 2 years. Therefore, Article 31 should be deleted. If necessary, you can keep Article 31 (1). No. 1 2 which gives Karens in connection with the fact that a researcher is known scientifically dishonest. It is then a supplementary punishment for the things that often come with when a researcher becomes known scientifically dishonest. One can discuss whether that is reasonable. But that is certainly an option. We would also like to mention that we think it is an improvement in section 33 that it says that in the case of a complaint you will have a right of appeal so that within four weeks you can appeal to the Minister. We also think that this is a positive change from the original draft. So we're all positive about this bill. It is, in many respects, a very good legislative proposal, and it has also been a good process for its creation. However, as I have said, we have a few proposals for possible changes which we believe will make it even better.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202110138	Thank you. Then it's the Minister of Education and Research. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170202110154	Thank you for the fine debate. The purpose of the bill is to establish a new unified and structured regulation of the Danish Research and Innovation Policy Council and the Danish Free Research Foundation. The bill is based on a political agreement of 13 October on the framework for the Danish Free Research Foundation between the conciliation parties behind the research council system. The draft law must be seen in conjunction with proposals for a law on scientific infidelity that we discussed before. Together, the two proposals will replace the Research Advisory Act which regulates the situation today. In its content, the proposal extends the framework of the Danish Research and Innovation Policy Council. The Danish Research and Innovation Policy Council will continue to aim to promote the development of Danish research, technology development and innovation for the benefit of society. The Council will provide independent and expert advice on research, technology development and innovation, and the framework for the Council will be largely renewed. The Free Research Council, which will be called the Danish Free Research Foundation, must therefore continue to aim to strengthen the quality of Danish research by providing grants for concrete research activities based on the researchers' own initiatives. As in the past, the Foundation also has the task of advising on research. In addition to the new name, an extended possibility is proposed to translate funds for thematic research through special appropriations and, in addition, there will be the possibility of establishing a waiting period for applicants as well as the competence of the Management Board of the Fund to appoint members and chairmen of the underlying committees. Against this background, I thank you for your comments and I look forward to the further consideration of the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202111257	Thank you very much to the chairman for her kindness. I am sorry that, due to the consultation, I was late. In 1937 the Danish Parliament adopted the first Nature Conservation Act, which ensured a 100 m protection line on the coast. The aim was to ensure against construction and to ensure access to the beach for all citizens, or as Prime Minister Thorvald Stauning put it in his closing speech: we are in one of the last hours in this matter if we want to save values that are in danger. Nature's salvation for the people means access to sources of beauty and health, and the Reichstag, which now possibly saves these great natural values from destruction, will earn from all landowners by at the same time preventing the sale of foolish narrow-mindedness to the detriment of descendants. In 1994, beach protection under the then Environment Minister Svend Auken in large parts of the country was extended from the original 100 m to 300 m. It is therefore unnecessary to point out that the issue of beach protection, coastal protection, is the heartblood of social democracy. The bill we are dealing with today translates the draft law agreement on Denmark into a better balance, which the Social Democracy entered into with the then Left Government, the Danish People's Party and the Conservative People's Party. It is no secret that the initiative of the then narrow Left Government contained far-reaching plans for our most precious nature, the open beaches in which my party could not see ourselves. I am therefore pleased that the negotiations succeeded in preventing the granting of permission to build hotels along our coasts, placing hundreds of beach chairs and a mess of other disgusting things on our beaches. The agreement shows why, with Hans Hansen's words, it makes sense always to be more concerned with results than resolutions, because it was in the negotiations that we achieved the results that now make a difference to some of the most precious nature that we have, namely our open beaches. I really think that the draft law has ensured a good balance between use and protection. It is a compromise, but I am satisfied with what we have achieved in the agreement on a better balance for Denmark. The bill opens up the possibility of port buildings which are no longer used being designed and put into service to support tourism, association and other activities in the ports. In addition, more relaxed rules are introduced as to what municipalities are allowed to do between city and port, and it will be possible to establish bathing ramps for people with disabilities in our ports. I think that is a good thing. The proposal thus contributes to increased activity in our ports and thereby to increased activity and growth in our local communities, often local communities in the more sparsely populated rural areas. The draft law also allows landowners with land within the beach protection line to establish smaller facilities such as high beds and play equipment, and provides for exemptions for outhouses and greenhouses up to 10 m2 The derogation depends, however, on the fact that the applied for does not significantly affect the coastal landscape on the site and that the area is not protected. With the proposal, we also relax the rules on the beach protection line, so that clubs, associations, volunteers and institutions will be better able to establish, for example, changing facilities, sheds for storing sea kayaks or fishing equipment, bonfire cabins, lunch cabins or shelters, if this happens in connection with existing beach facilities, such as a detour to the beach or a parking lot. At the same time, it is a requirement that the activities to be supported by the facilities opened up have a strong connection with the water, and it is also a requirement that the facilities be placed as withdrawn as possible, so that the large open coastlines are always maintained as far as possible, and that the facilities are described in a local plan so that they fall as far as possible into the nature and landscape of the site. The new possibilities provide a better framework for outdoor life; they strengthen our wide-ranging association life and encourage more people to enter the wild. This is positive for our association life, for our lives in local communities, but it is of course also positive for health, as Stauning also mentioned. The proposal as it stands strikes a good balance between the protection of the open coasts and the ability of local communities to unfold in nature. There is a good balance, I think, between the opportunities offered by the tourist industry and the protection of nature. It is important for business and tourism to have good opportunities and a framework, but it is also important to remember that it is precisely the unspoiled nature and our open coasts which in these municipalities close to the water have the greatest attraction to tourists. With those words, I can say that the Social Democratic Party can support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202115113	Thank you. As we have already mentioned in the debate so far, it was originally the intention of the Left Government to open up more construction in the open, free Danish coastal nature. It was the government's proposal that there should be more pilot projects within the beach protection line, just as it was in the rural area of the Left that the coastal proximity zone would be abolished and the beach protection line would be moved to the nearest house or public road. More than hundreds of thousands of Danes participated in a petition against the element that was about several experimental projects within the beach protection line. Thousands of people ringed the Danish coasts with torches and fires, and the message from the population was quite clear: the coasts are one of the most valuable things we have, and there should not be built in the last free nature we have in Denmark. It's one of the most unspoiled that's left. Here from the Folketing there was actually listened, at least to a certain extent. There was no support for the government's plans, and I think it is appropriate to sign both the Social Democracy and the Conservatives, who were determined to preserve the beach protection line, which protects against water-side construction and 300 metres inland, and thus refused to open up more experimental construction. Today, then, we are dealing with the legislative proposal that has to translate part of the political agreement, and although I would like to emphasise that the worst disasters have clearly been prevented, we still believe that the draft law is a step in the wrong direction because, in a number of areas, the protection of our invaluable coastal nature is being reduced and reduced. The bill opens up more building from the home or company than today, for real local planning on the beaches and for the abolition of protection on selected routes. We are concerned that the draft law proposes that it should be much easier to build on the beaches, especially along the west coast of Jutland, and down to the shores of the beach. I would like to be quite honest and say that when I read this bill, I think it is a little unclear how the rules will exactly be implemented. We need to get more light on this in the legislative process if the Minister for the Environment is not able to explain it more precisely when he goes to the platform later. However, this is partly due to the fact that the specific rules, which the Social Democratic Party also mentioned, must be translated into later announcements. However, I think that when we deal with a bill in the Folketing, it is quite important that we have the opportunity to see through what it can actually mean for the nature that I think many of us are very concerned about protecting. For example: I have not been able to find a concrete figure on the size of the area involved in abolishing the beach protection line between town and port, just as I think it is unclear exactly where we can expect more settlement and to what extent. For us, the Danish coasts and habitat types that exist along the coasts, whether dunes, dunes or lows, are in an international context one of the most valuable nature we have in Denmark. And I would like to emphasise this, just as I think it is so important to remember the background to the beach protection line. It goes all the way back to the 1930s, where you saw how construction spread down the beach, so that it reduced the ability of the broad population to experience a rich and unspoiled nature and to walk along the beach, along the coast, and just let the gaze slide into the horizon without having to look into a shed or toilet or all sorts of other things. That is what is behind it, and I still believe that today there is a need to stick to it, so that what we are protecting is the interests of the community and the general public. And then it is true that one then limits the opportunity for the few to think about the opportunity for the many. In the Enhedslisten we cannot vote for the proposal in its present form, but we need to ask a lot of questions about it. We also need, I think, to table a number of amendments, because we believe that a better balance can be struck between those things and so disability toilets in ice-cream, etc., without giving as much relief as this bill gives. I also think it is important to say that when you delve into the comments of the bill, you have to bear in mind that in a great many, in the vast majority of cases we have discussed, such as apple trees in a garden, a sandbox or a bed, it has been authorised if you have a legal garden. So we should also be careful not to make this problem bigger than it is. Thank you.

Then we believe that the licence holder must be accountable at all levels in such an attempt and for all those involved. I have referred to page 14 that it says that objective liability is no longer generally applicable, and it is not given in advance that the authorisation to lay down rules on objective liability will actually be used for that. The driver of a car in an experimental scheme is now being placed at a much greater risk, in our opinion, of being held responsible. Did he have time to intervene? In calculating the time that has elapsed, concepts such as reasonable time and a shorter period of time are used. What's that in seconds? We believe that far too far-reaching consideration has been given to possible applicants for an experimental scheme and too far-reaching a framework has been laid down which provides the basis for interpretation. I thought that was a good way to do it. It is also in line with the Bureau Conference, which we had recently, that this form can also be used in the committees. ParlaMint-DK_20170202144909	Thank you. There are no brief comments. Since no more rapporteurs have asked to speak, it is the Minister for Transport, Building and Housing.
ParlaMint-DK_20170202144919	Thank you. The Traffic Act is clear about the driver's role when driving a car with self-propelled functions: the car is allowed to drive itself, but as a driver you must have exactly the same attention to the traffic as when driving normally. With the traffic law's demands for an active driver, automation can make the journey more comfortable for the driver and automation can help make traffic safer. However, the requirements of the Road Traffic Act to the driver restrict the innovation we can achieve through the use of self-propelled cars, buses and lorries. It also makes very good sense, on the other hand, that traffic law protects citizens from immature technologies which can be dangerous to us when we are in traffic. This is the right balance between precisely the two considerations that we would like to strike with the proposal for a pilot scheme for self-propelled cars. On the one hand, we want to maintain control and, on the other hand, we want to open up new opportunities. It is relatively simple to imagine how practical it could be to get the car to drive the children to swimming or football. And there are companies abroad who firmly believe that the taxi of the future is self-propelled. In general, however, it is far too early to determine where and how we are going to notice the effects of automation in the transport sector. Against this backdrop, we are proposing an experimental system which could serve as a basis for various experiments on self-propelled cars. The pilot scheme will open up controlled tests on self-propelled cars. The bill shows what we mean by a controlled experiment: the cars in the experiment must be approved, the test lines must be precisely defined in advance, and there must be an assessor who agrees with road safety. However, there is a limit to how much control we can put down over an experiment. As with all other technology, there will always be a risk of technical defects in the case of self-propelled cars. The careful authorisation procedure shall minimise this risk and possible adverse effects. On the other hand, with self-propelled cars, we can avoid human error much better. It is the balance between the consequences of new technical errors and the consequences of avoiding human error, which will determine how fast progress towards self-propelled cars can be made. Overall, I believe that the government's proposal for an experimental scheme has found the right balance between security and innovation. It will provide a good framework for Denmark to become a place where companies and authorities test and show what self-propelled cars can be used for as technology becomes ready. Finally, I would like to thank once again for the positive comments which I hope will also be maintained during the work of the committee to the extent that a broad majority can be formed for the legislative proposal. Then I would like to add a few comments to what Mr. Henning Hyllested has raised about responsibility: Who is responsible? It is very simple and not as mysterious as I think Mr. Henning Hyllested has made it in this debate. When you are allowed to run an experiment on self-propelled cars, liability is always the responsibility of the licence holder. Punishment is also always the responsibility of the licence holder in cases where the car is actually self-propelled. However, when we open up that it can be in a different way in defined cases, it is because we can well imagine that in some experiments, that is, as part of the experiment, cars will be included, which we already know today, that is, with the self-driving functions we know today, where the driver is committed to watching the traffic and is committed to having his hands on or close to the steering wheel. They can be part of the experimental arrangements and when it is the case that the car is driven by a driver • e.g. the cars we already know, which can stay within the stripes and can keep distance from the front, etc. etc. That is already the case today. However, where there are going to be changes, there is where we might be allowed to • if someone is applying for it, and if they can meet the requirements of the applications • that the driver does not have to pay attention to the traffic all the time, or that there does not have to be a driver present in the car, but instead has to come to an accident if there are problems. In these cases, the permit holder will be liable for the penalty, but if there are parts of the experiment where the driver is sitting with the wheel in his hand in a car with a self-propelled technology that we already have on the roads today, it is the driver who is responsible for the penalty. That must be the case, and I do not think it can be otherwise. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202151304	The minister stumbles on his way up to the platform Yep, there I was. I strongly recommend that the motion for a resolution be rejected. There are several reasons for this. Firstly, the decision to build a smaller part of the Amager Common goes back a long way. Specifically, it was in 1992 that the Danish Parliament, in connection with the foundation of the Ørestadsselskabet I/S, decided for the first time that Ørestaden should be developed. Later in 2007 in connection with the foundation Metro Company and City & Port, the Danish Parliament decided that City & Port should enter into all the rights and duties of the Ørestads company's area development activities. The law was passed by a broad majority. Only the Enhedslisten voted against. The development of Ørestaden is not new either in Copenhagen's municipal plan. The Ørestaden was mentioned for the first time in a municipality plan in 1991 and since then continuously in 1996, 1997, 2001, 2005, 2009, 2011 and most recently in the Municipality Plan 2015, which states that the area can be expanded from 2012. Secondly, in 1992, when it was decided that the Ørestaden should be developed, it was also decided that an Ørestad Council should be set up, which among other things: should ensure that natural values are taken into account. The Ørestad Council, which among other things: consisted of representatives of the Danish Nature Conservation Association and the Free Air Council, had a significant influence on the design of the overall plan for Ørestaden and thus on the fulfilment of the area's natural values. A comprehensive plan for Ørestaden was created in 1995 on the basis of an international architectural competition. The part of the Amager Common originally covered by the Ørestad area was 86 ha. After the involvement of the Ørestad Council, the project area was narrowed to only 18.7 ha, corresponding to only 22 per cent. of the originally intended building field of the Amager Common. At the same time it was agreed on the location of the building field, namely in the corner of Vejlands Allé and Ørestads Boulevard. It was also agreed to free the most valuable wetlands, including Lake Grønjord, from settlement. The Danish Parliament then adopted an amendment to the Ørestadsloven, so that the natural area around the project area and the area around Lake Grønjord cannot be built, but is only used for nature care and the establishment of paths. The project area that we are talking about today and which, among other things, is: The Danish Nature Conservation Association has been involved in recommending, so in order to accommodate nature has already been severely narrowed. In comparison, I can mention that the area is approximately 6%. By Amager Common, approx. 1% by Kalvebod Fælled and ca. 0,5% by Nature Park Amager, which by the way is three times larger than Dyrehaven. So there will remain a very large natural area once the project area has been built. The municipality of Copenhagen has prepared a development plan with recommendations for recreational opportunities and nature care for Amager Fælled and the northern natural area in Ørestaden. The recommendations in this development plan follow the City & Port, of course. City & Port will include: take a number of measures to improve the living conditions of the internationally protected amphibians in order to maintain their ecological function in the area. The measures mainly concern the establishment of new water holes and the purification of existing water holes outside the project area, as well as ensuring a passage between the breeding sites of the amphibians north and south of the project area. As regards protected and rare plants, in the further preparation of the master plan for the area, City & Port will assess how common nature can be an important element in the new urban area. City & Port is therefore very aware of the nature of the project area, including both the protected nature and the rarer nature. Last but not least, of course, there is also the question of the economy. If the area is not developed as provided, it will result in a significant gap in terms of a billion euros in the City & Port's economy. The company City & Port was born with a significant debt of 9.2 billion. kr. The company has thus had to contribute upfront to the financing of the Copenhagen metro, including both the current metro and Cityringen, in the expectation that over the years City & Port can manage the land development of its land and buildings which, through subsequent sales, can help to reduce the debt. After the foundation, the company also had to contribute to the financing of a number of other major infrastructure projects, including the Nordhavn Metro, the South Harbour Metro and the Nordhavn Tunnel. The company currently has a net debt of approx. 15 billion kr. A key condition for reducing this debt is that there is no erosion of the company's property portfolio, including the Ørestad Common Quarter project area. The infrastructure projects that we all already benefit from and benefit from today are therefore largely financed through payments from the City & Port. Payments have been made. In addition, the company has an obligation to finance, where appropriate, a budget reserve of approx. 2 billion kr. concerning the South Harbour Metro. For its part, therefore, the company has a larger debt to be settled through the sale of land as provided by the Folketing. From the outset, there has been a broad majority in favour of this model. Now we also have a joint responsibility to ensure that the framework for debt resolution is maintained. We can't just run off the bill for an hour or so. The solutions mentioned in the motion for a resolution are not even on the ground. Firstly, a gap in the City & Port's economy cannot be easily converted into an extension of the repayment period for the Metro company's debts. These are two separate legal entities. Furthermore, the debt of the Metro Company has not been repaid in 2065 in advance. That does not argue for a further extension. Secondly, the City & Port does not have other areas in Ørestaden for residential purposes except approx. 120,000 floor meters in Ørestad South. In addition, the remaining area of the co-owner, owned by the State and the City of Copenhagen respectively, is protected and therefore cannot be built. In addition, it has been a planned prerequisite for the development of the Ørestaden and the metro that Ørestad Common Quarter is developed within the decided project area at Amager Common to create coherence and unity with the other Ørestaden as well as proximity to the metro connection and the already established metro station Sundby Station. The Enhedslisten and the Alternative mention in their motion for a resolution that it is never too late to reverse a wrong decision. I very much agree with that. But I do not believe that the construction of the Amager Common, including the Ørestad Common Quarter, is a wrong decision and, with the broad majority that has helped to make the decision at the time, I think that I am not alone in that position. The decision on the construction of the Amager Common dates back to the early 1990s, and from the outset the green organisations, the Danish Nature Conservation Association and the Free Air Council, have already been involved in the decision on the concrete location of the project area, so that the nature of the Ørestaden was taken into account as much as possible. The concerns that may be about nature in the very limited project area have therefore already been taken into account and addressed once. The motion for a resolution should therefore be rejected.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202170808	Thank you very much. Amager Common is unique. If you live in the middle of Copenhagen, you can jump on a bike, and shortly afterwards you will be standing out on an area of Amager Common, where you will now be building. If you stand in the building field, you will find, if you look, that you are standing in no less than a so-called biodiversity hotspot. In concrete terms, this means that you can be fortunate enough to see rare species of birds such as carrots, thornsongs and grasshopper singers, and if you look carefully, you may find the endangered plant wood screen that has one of its last habitats in Denmark on this particular area. And if you sit down, there is a good chance that you might see some of the more than 20 species of native wild beach plants and perhaps some of the 7 endangered fungus species that only exist here in the City of Copenhagen. If you build here, you risk some of these plants and species disappear. In other words, you are standing on the Amager Common, not on some random green area when you are standing on the building field. You are standing in a unique area, and that is because it is an area that has not been built or cultivated for 5,000 years. It is a 5,000-year-old beach beach and thus one of the very last remains of original nature in the City of Copenhagen. The rest of Amager Common is filled soil from an old dump, but this very area is special. And why are we in this really crazy situation that you want to place apartment blocks on some of the finest nature of the City of Copenhagen? This is because Copenhagen, back in the early 1990s when the subway was to be financed, chose to do so by means of basic sales, including by the sale of Amager Samled. And a lot has happened fortunately, one can say in 25 years. We are now much more aware of how important open green areas are, how important it is to find peace and experience nature, especially when living in a hectic city like Copenhagen. And we can see today, also if we lift our eyes and look beyond Denmark and the nature we have here, that Amager Samled is something very special. In a national context, too, we are dealing with a very unique nature here, and that is why the decision taken back in 1992 should, of course, be reconsidered. When the decision that Amager Common could be used as a building site was made, there was much less understanding of what nature close to the city could actually mean, nor did it know that 25 years later we were in a situation where we are so far from meeting the basic objectives of halting the loss of nature and biodiversity in Denmark, that there is no chance that we will achieve our goal of halting the destruction of nature by 2020. And then, of course, there is nature, which there should be no doubt about is completely unique and invaluable. If today in 2017 we were to make this decision and really decided that the most unique nature on the Amager Common should be built, people would undoubtedly think that we were crazy. And that is exactly where you should take responsibility and stop and say: maybe it wasn't the best decision, maybe we should check whether there are alternatives. Instead, one pulls on the shoulder and says that done is done and places responsibility for this failed decision with politicians who made the decision many years ago. I would also like to stress that this is a matter for the Folketing, because it is a law in here that needs to be amended in order for this to be possible. I hope that will become clear to most people. Then it is clear that alternative funding will have to be found if it is that this land must be kept free of excavators, and there have been various proposals for alternative building sites on the table, but so far it has been stranded in the municipality, as some council members have not found the proposals for alternative funding realistic. ParlaMint-DK_20170202172802	The debate is open, and the first is Mr. Thomas Jensen, the Social Democratic Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170202172826	Thank you, President. In November 2015, the then Left Government came up with a very far-reaching proposal for the liberalisation of the Planning Act. The proposal would have had dramatic consequences both for our open shores and unspoiled beaches and for the lives of our communities. Yes, the Left Government would even put an end to cheap housing in the larger cities. Social democracy entered into negotiations with the government over a long period of time, tough negotiations, but fortunately it ended with a result that managed to pull its teeth out of the government's most extensive liberalisations. And it is clear that this is the case with conciliation that not all the elements of the final agreement have grown in the social democratic garden, but of course we stand by the conciliation we are making and the agreement we are now discussing. I think that the parties behind the broad settlement have all ended up with an agreement that adjusts the law of plans so that, on the one hand, it improves the opportunities for growth, development and settlement throughout Denmark, and, on the other hand, at the same time protects the Danish peculiarity, which is a good environment and a rich nature. And I would like to thank the other parties behind the agreement for that. The legislative proposal we are discussing here will then give effect to the agreement, and I will not go into the details of the legislative proposal, but will emphasise that there are a number of very good measures that create a better framework for growth. The bill opens the way for growth and development opportunities for the productive industries, tourism and retail trade, including in rural areas. And as the law is today, there is often pressure on production companies due to increased environmental demands and conflicts, which means that a company cannot expand the company in its current location. This means that the company has to move, and this is often a costly affair. Therefore, this draft law in the field of industry is limited to the production industries, so that existing production industries are ensured that they do not have to meet new requirements at the present location. And that will then provide increased investment security for many manufacturing companies. It is so important to stress here that these are not less stringent environmental requirements for companies, but simply that the existing companies are not subject to new and additional environmental requirements for the location they currently have. The draft law creates the possibility of ensuring a more efficient shop structure. The municipalities must ensure this by providing space for larger shops, new store concepts that can ensure a better range of products and lower prices. And the question then is whether it will lead to large shops and many small towns without shops. And here it is important for municipalities to ensure that there is a varied supply of shops in small and medium-sized cities and good accessibility for all types of road users to these shops. With a more flexible use of the summer houses during the year, more life and a longer tourism season will be ensured in Waterside Denmark. This seems to us to be quite positive. The law also makes it more attractive to settle in rural areas. On the basis of the consultation phase, it has been made possible for municipalities to grant land-zone permits for the establishment of several dwellings in redundant buildings. This will increase the settlement opportunities, and that is very good. All in all, it is a series of measures that together allow citizens and businesses and local authorities to achieve balanced growth throughout Denmark. We Social Democrats are pleased to be involved in this. What has social democracy secured in these negotiations? Social democracy fought in the negotiations for housing to be available for the SOSU assistant and police officer in our cities in the future. And here the Left Government would, of course, remove the possibility in the planning law for municipal councils to be able to impose a requirement that there should be up to 25%. general housing in the context of local planning for new residential areas. Fortunately, that didn't happen. We also took the breath out of the previous government's desire to experiment with building hotels and other things along our coasts and on our common lands. We have secured new development opportunities for the municipalities with the new development zones, so that it supports more life and growth throughout Denmark, but without buying out our valuable nature. At the same time, we have ensured more and better coherent nature. We do this by preserving and strengthening the green Danish map so that it is now a central tool in the municipalities' work to create a much more coherent nature. We are very aware that with the bill here we are adjusting many places. Some citizens have been pleased with the new opportunities offered by the agreement, and others think that a tooth may have been adjusted too much. Indeed, spatial planning affects all of us and is of great concern to many Danes, and this is also reflected in the 400-page consultation responses that have been received. That is why I would also like to emphasise that it is an important part of the agreement that it will be evaluated by July 2020, so that we have a hand in dealing with any inappropriateness in the current proposal. In conclusion, we welcome the fact that this is a balanced legislative proposal for amending the law on plans. We are pleased that a compromise has been reached where we have all given ourselves a little, and we Social Democrats, of course, when we are in the agreement, can also support this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202174432	L 121 is a single legislative proposal which contains a large number of different sub-elements in relation to the reorganisation of the existing law on plans. After all, it has resulted in this agreement, a broad political agreement, Denmark in better balance sheet, which we concluded with the then government, the Social Democrats and also the Conservative People's Party back in June 2016. We can now look forward to a number of improved opportunities for municipalities and local political government. In future, municipal councils will bear greater political responsibility, but will also have a greater degree of competence to deal with spatial planning. It will continue to take place in an open democratic process in which proposals for plans, etc. should be put forward for public consultation and local debate so that all views can also continue to emerge. There are various key elements to this bill. The Danish People's Party particularly welcomes the improved development opportunities we have been given for our production and development in our rural areas, better use of existing construction in rural areas and also in coastal areas. Then we also think it is good that we should have a freer framework for investment and administrative relief and that we should also do better in relation to tourism. ParlaMint-DK_20170202180923	Thank you very much. We have a bill in our hands for a new law of plans which, fortunately, does not fully reflect the thoughts of the far-reaching liberalisation of the law of plans, which was part of the act of the previous government, the V government, the growth and development of the whole of Denmark. You can see in this bill some positive impressions from some of the conciliation parties, and we would like to sign for that. In the Enhedslisten we particularly welcome the fact that plans for a green Denmark map have come alive. Denmark has not only some of the smallest natural area in Europe, but also some of the most poor nature. That is why we are pleased that this work is now being set in motion. But here the positive messages from the Enhedslisten stop too. Because the proposal still contains a number of very worrying elements for our nature and for our rural areas. In general terms, we believe that the draft law is in fact a dispute with what the law of plans has represented for so many years. This is perhaps one of the best planning laws in the world with this idea that planning should be a key tool for creating better cohesion in our country. Several elements of this bill will, in our opinion, mean less cohesion. More elements will not lift up rural areas, on the contrary. The bill could have a huge impact on our coasts. The government breaks the promise to preserve the coastal proximity zone and introduces a new concept called the development area. These are areas in the coastal proximity zone where urban development and facilities may be available and where the coastal proximity zone may be requested every four years. This can have quite major consequences for our coastal landscapes over time, as coastal nature can become much more fragmented and mean a greater degree of settlement. It may upset us a little that the detailed rules under which this will be administered have not yet been published. We find it rather problematic that we should deal with a legislative proposal without having the opportunity to see through what the consequences will actually be for the coastal nature of the country. The liberalisation of land-zone rules, as proposed by the bill, means, among other things, that the land-zone rules are changed so that both housing and professions can in future expand existing buildings by up to 500 m2, without any form of authorisation being sought, and without the municipality's approval and without neighbouring consultation. We quite disagree with the fact that carte blanche can only be given for the expansion of large homes and commercial properties without any kind of permission. We simply do not understand why this liberalisation is allowed to apply to all rural areas whose aim is to help the needy rural areas and to bring about life and growth in the country. The problem is that these new rules are likely to be used, where the resources are available, where the desire for investment and loan possibilities are best. This means that it will typically be in rural areas in the capital region and around other major cities in the country that we will see that these rules will be used. This means that these changes will benefit the urban areas far more than the rural areas in need which the agreement aims to help. It will only increase the disparities between urban areas and other rural areas. This bill is in no way aimed at creating a Denmark in balance, which the former government, the Left Government, has so diligently used as an argument. The current draft law can also make use of the relaxations in the rural area of the capital region, and this will mean that there is a risk of a dispute with the Finger Plan, which has so far ensured that the capital region does not grow arbitrarily in all directions, but grows in the form of a palm with fingers, with good train connections, etc. This is not appropriate as the land zone of the capital region is heavily under pressure for both housing and business construction. And that is what we have already discussed with the Socialist rapporteur and which we will discuss in greater detail during the committee procedure in order to gain a more concrete overview of what this is going to mean. Another element that concerns us is the new rules on retail trade, which I also mentioned in my question to the Left. As with the amended country-zone rules, it seems that the new retail rules are going to run counter to the purpose of the bill to strengthen opportunities for growth and employment and settlement throughout Denmark, because it will make it possible to put relief centres and retail stores outside the cities more effectively, thereby putting cities at risk of their shopping life being drained. Overall, I can say that we are very concerned about the consequences of this bill on the coastal nature of the country, and we do not understand how the government and the conciliation parties can declare the bill as a lever to strengthen the opportunities for growth and employment. We can't support this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202181932	The alternative will vote against the proposal. After all, it is a good idea to work towards the development of urban areas, not least in the smaller towns and rural areas. In the alternative, we are very concerned about creating a more coherent Denmark or a Denmark in better balance, as the parties to the agreement call it. We acknowledge that it may be relevant to look at the law of plans in the process and would very much like to acknowledge at first that the green Danish map has come in. However, if we are against it, it is because we believe that this proposal has taken a wrong overall view of the draft law. We believe that it contains a number of steps in the wrong direction. We are liberalising the planning rules and continuing to free agriculture from planning frameworks, and we are also changing the purpose of the planning law. After all, the planning law has always had a very good purpose to balance social interests, especially in terms of nature, the environment and landscape value. With the bill, the government has wanted to expand the purpose of demands for economic growth. At a time when it is more important than ever that we remove the focus from short-term economic growth and look at things in a slightly larger perspective, I am surprised that an agreement that should be about balance will, at the same time, have a goal of economic growth. Fortunately, the wording has been preserved with respect for nature, the environment and the landscape, but on the other hand, it provides for a revision and liberalisation of the land-zone rules. Last time the land-zone rules were relaxed, special rural regulations were introduced so that the relaxed rules could only be used in particularly disadvantaged rural areas, which were then intended to help with the relaxations. Now the whole country is being relaxed and the special rural regulations are being removed. Outside the major cities and rural areas, there are many challenges posed by declining employment and a weak housing market. I fully acknowledge that and it is also something that we in the Alternative believe something needs to be done about. However, with the amendments I have just mentioned here, it is spreading so widely that there is no longer any special aid for the disadvantaged regions. In other words, this means favouring the areas of resource strength rather than highlighting the areas under threat. It's a really bad idea. It appears that the aim is to make it more attractive to live and live in rural areas, but making the rules equal for all parts of the country rather than allowing them only to apply in these particular rural areas will happen the opposite. The reliefs also apply to the capital region of those parts of the green wedges which are landzones. It is potentially a disaster for the landscapes of the capital, where the planners are already fighting a brave battle to ensure landscape considerations. In the past, the region of the capital has been excluded from relaxed land-zone rules, and this should, of course, continue to be done. One thing I would like to address is the special status of agriculture in the planning act. In accordance with the rules in force, agriculture shall have the right to construct the buildings necessary for its operation as agricultural property. This may require a permit, in relation to where the building is located, if it is longer than 20 metres from existing settlements, but now the rules are extended to apply not only to a property but to a holding. This means that if a farmer has a holding consisting of five properties, he can without permission build his buildings on the same property for as long as he can prove that he needs the buildings somewhere on the farm. At the same time, the Planning Act states that no local plans may be made for agricultural buildings. This has always been the case because agriculture has had a special position, but this should be done when the buildings have grown so large and at the same time the concept of property is abolished, so that the buildings can now be spread all over the place or, vice versa, assembled into a huge mass of buildings in one place. Then there should be a demand for local planning so that a clear framework can be created for the location of buildings, how they should look, etc., in the same way as for all other professions. In the alternative, we can easily see the need for an adjustment of the law of plans when it comes to agriculture. We would like to adjust so that it opens up a sustainable settlement of the agricultural land and puts a damper on the agricultural factory building, which is being set up here. We will, for example: to look instead at opportunities to promote permaculture agriculture and forestry and the kind that is part of the future. In relation to the retail trade rules, I am quite concerned about the larger shops, because this will increase the inappropriate development of cities losing shops because they are run out by large shopping centres outside the cities. And then it is quite interesting when Dansk Detail writes in their hearing answer: "Overall, it is our view that the retail regulations in this bill open up so many uncertainties, uncertainties and interpretations that there is a considerable risk of a significant number of complaints and legal proceedings in order to clarify the retail regulations. The fact that individual cases end up being a cornerstone of the legislation in an otherwise well-functioning legislation with this change of law may be something that we in the Danish Parliament should consider carefully before we go in that direction. Finally, I would like to highlight the response from Viktor Reddersen, because this is in fact in line with what we are working for in the alternative. He writes: "Today the law does not allow the new one - that there is more than one housing unit in a land estate. You can then apply for a land zone permit and possibly be allowed to expand by a maximum of 250 square metres in one more housing unit. We ask whether it is not time to look at whether that limitation should be lifted or maybe completely removed when we talk about existing operating buildings • By relaxing quite cautiously on the planning law, you would allow the rural areas to rethink the farms for settlement communities, opportunities for integration of refugees, collectives or even generations in the same place. New opportunities rather than decay. With this quote, I will round off and repeat that the alternative cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202182605	Thank you very much. We sometimes discuss the law of plans, and I think it is worth saying that it does not have a very good name, because the law of plans will easily sound like something like that with a planned economic attack on the population. And in reality, the more we are talking about a law of consideration that takes into account many interests that must be in the same country, or a kind of cohesion law for how we can make our country hang together, or a law that deals with how we can make cities stay cities and be alive and preserve our open country less built-in and more open and free, so that people can also choose to settle down places where there is no scattered settlement all over the place. That's why I've issued a fatwa earlier in my life about the word "planlov," but I haven't really figured out the really good name. I sometimes think it is very easy to make it sound as if those who are in favour of planning do not like freedom and as if those who oppose planning and the law of plans are enormously lovers of freedom. But you always have to remember that one's freedom is the other's lack of freedom, right? So you can give a citizen a lot of rights, as you do with this bill, but then there are some neighbors and some considerations that are not taken into account. So I would like to say that it has been a very interesting process to follow this planetary act that started with the fact that the Left really gave it gas. And it was probably a strategy to propose just to remove everything, because it could then be that it ended somewhere in between. Then, of course, quite sensible forces came into the debate and I would like to commend both the Conservatives and the Social Democrats for having the country a planned law agreement that is, in fact, very, very sensible. The part we are talking about today is in the direction of giving the municipalities some more responsibility for making plans for themselves which parts need to be developed, among other things. in the coastal zone. I think that is a good idea, especially if we can sort out more clearly what the state interests are, and perhaps also say to the municipalities that it may well be that it is not every four years, but every ten years, that they must have an opportunity to include new land within an overall plan. At any rate, it is a good idea, like taking the consequences of the municipal reform that we have had, to say: well, there is more influence, and therefore there must also be more responsibility for the municipalities, but in return, the interests of the state and what requires state planning must also be clear. So I actually think there are some good steps in this direction. I also think it is sensible enough to cancel 5,000 reservations for cottages and make room for 6,000 new ones. It is thus some reservations that have been there for many years and are not really used and then you might be able to use it to give some opportunities elsewhere, without necessarily meaning much more construction. Then I would like to say that the thing that concerns me most is, of course, the one that concerns the relaxed land-zone rules, which should now apply throughout the country. The problem is that the municipalities are losing a very great deal of influence, and they also write in their hearing answer that it is a paradox that during a period when you want to give the municipalities more influence, you actually deprive them of the opportunity to plan for this by giving citizens an immediate right to expand to 500 m2 of accommodation or take away not just a abandoned agricultural property, but any property, and make up to 500 m2 of business in it. Then we had a discussion with the Social Democrat rapporteur on whether the country-zone rules which have been relaxed here will also apply in the wedges of the Finger Plan. This has been clarified, and it can be read in the hearings that they do. These rules will apply in the wedges, there are no particular considerations there, and that means that there will be much more scattered settlements in the green wedges in the Copenhagen area, with meaning for everything that is the foundation of the Finger Plan, namely that for 70 years it has been said that there must be something special in these green zones. They are also under extra pressure, because it is interesting to get close to the metropolitan area, but a little cheaper than inside the cities. And that is what worries me: that industry will start moving into these areas because it is a little cheaper than being in industrial areas. I do not think this rule is going to benefit the whole country, but it is going to mean a great deal where there are already towns and where industry is going to lie a little outside industrial areas. And it also makes it more difficult for municipalities to manage things. Furthermore, there may be a problem for agriculture that if someone has an immediate right to expand, there are suddenly noise rules or smell rules which may cause problems for agricultural enlargement. So it is a very good example that giving a single person some freedom can cost a lot of other people some freedom. I know that in Frederiksborg County Newspaper the other day you could read about a family that up in Lynge has built a house in the assumption that they were in a green wedge and had invested in it, but that now with the new law... if you pass this... there's an upcycling center right next to them, right next to where they've invested in a new house in this free area, where there's going to be 500 trucks a day. And they cannot do anything; they do not even have an opposition right. I think this is a very good example of how it is very cool to give a freedom to one citizen, but what about all the neighbours? What about a municipality that has to secure suitable infrastructure? Is that wise? That is why I would like to put out a hand from the Radical Left and say that if we can have this part looked at; that if we can get the influence back to the municipalities; that if we can look at this immediate right with new eyes and perhaps ask whether it is wise to apply it in the area of the Finger Plan, then we actually think it would be enough to vote for this whole bill too. So we go constructively to it, but have this very, very serious concern.

ParlaMint-DK_20170202183146	This bill is called a modernisation of the planning law, but it isn't. Modernisation will require an in-depth analysis and analysis with a view to carrying out a thorough review of the Planning Act. Today, the law is very incalculable as a result of the many changes that have been made since it was first introduced over 40 years ago. In some respects, the law on plans is no longer in line with the more fundamental changes that have taken place in society since then. Let me just mention the demands for necessary reduction in emissions of climate gases, reduced use of all kinds of resources and the need for climate adaptation of our coasts and cities. These are needs which should not only be addressed in sectoral legislation, but which must be considered in the overall planning of the development of our nature and the built-up environment. So the essence of this bill is the liberalisation of the law of plans. It's about limiting society's and citizens' influence on the country's development and planning, why not be honest instead of newspeak, spin, using positive words about modernisation. Mayors, peasants and builders must have better conditions, the framework must be loosened, and the local authorities can, with the change of purpose, give greater priority to growth and development than before, as stated in the comments to the amended paragraph of purpose. This is to be seen in the context of the Law on the Board of Appeal, which was adopted by a majority before Christmas, which quotes • "to reflect a better balance between the considerations of growth and development in respect of nature and the environment. However, development and growth have always been essential elements in the planning of the municipalities, and there are many examples of priority being given to growth and development over the consideration of nature and the environment. It is a bureaucrat formulation which is so elastic that it does not add anything to the often reused formulation that quotes continue to be protected by the country's nature and environment. None of the wordings ensures that the bill improves the environment, nature or biodiversity. The question could then be asked: why not, for once, promote nature, the environment and biodiversity? The general comments stress the importance of public involvement in planning. However, with the reduction of the period from 8 to 4 weeks and in some cases 2 weeks, the possibility of ordinary people and associations making comments is effectively removed. The pressure for shorter consultation periods does not come from the municipal environment, but is created by parts of the business community who cannot understand that it really must take so long to adopt a local plan proposal which they often perceive as clapped off with Technical Management in advance. Thus, a very lack of acceptance of the democratic right of citizens to participate in the planning process. Then there are questions of legal certainty or lack of it. A law is, of course, a legal document which the authorities would like to provide with the necessary clarity. However, as can be seen from the comments submitted by the combined merchants, this bill is far from being the case. On the contrary, the use of language in the legislative text creates considerable legal uncertainty and that uncertainty cannot be clarified by any body of appeal, since only legal issues can be the subject of an appeal. This seems to create the basis for significantly different relationships between the municipality and the municipality. The citizen's access to appeal is for example. questions about the compatibility of local plan proposals with the requirements of the Local Plan Act, and the new language in the legislative text will make an appeal more difficult. It may sound very boring, so it is often with legal certainty that it sounds boring until you do not have it anymore. Let me conclude by commenting on the main message that the government and the parties to the agreement are making over and over again, namely that this creates rural development. This proposal is going to drain the retail trade of small towns and villages, simply. I would just like to highlight a few of them here: better lending opportunities in the outermost regions, support for micro-enterprises and entrepreneurship, more support for demolition, i.e. the demolition pool, shared municipal tax, so that you share between your local community and, for example, the local community. its summer cottage municipality, several educational institutions in the outermost regions, a regional hour model for trains and buses and an impact assessment of all legislative proposals in relation to their impact on rural areas. There are none of these proposals that you have wanted to support, although it would mean significantly more and make a world of difference for rural and smaller cities • contrary to this proposal. So the SF cannot support the present bill on the Planning Act.
